Eq. (3) characterizes average behavior. In operation, the instanta-
neous demand is not known a priori, so demand-induced deviations 
from this average relationship act as disturbances that must 
be rejected by feedback control. The scatter in Fig. 5 reflects 
this stochasticity.
This static head loss versus discharge relation is analogous to 
flow-modulation curves used in open-loop or locally controlled 
PRVs, where valve setting is adjusted as a function of inlet dis-
charge. Creaco and Walski (2018) show that, due to stochastic 
demand, a single discharge–valve-setting relationship implemented 
in a PLC cannot generally guarantee service-pressure constraints 
at all nodes and times. This limitation motivates the remote-node 
pressure feedback adopted here.
Pressure Dynamic Model of the Remote Node
To identify the dynamic model linking the PRV pilot command to 
the remote-node pressure head, we use step-test data (Fig. 6). The 
input is expressed as xp(t) (%), the normalized pilot downstream 
setpoint defined in Eq. (1). With the adopted 0–100 m H2O nor-
malization, 1% in xp(t) corresponds to 1 m H2O in the physical 
downstream setpoint hd, sp(t); thus, setpoints of 17 and 45 m H2O
correspond to xp = 17% and xp = 45%, respectively.
Each experiment was conducted under different instantaneous 
demand and upstream head, yielding different operating-point 
steady states. To obtain a model independent of these variations, we 
express responses in deviation variables by subtracting the pre-step 
steady value and time-aligning all experiments so that the valve step 
occurs at t = 0. The resulting deviation responses overlap closely, 
indicating consistent PRV–network dynamics over the tested range. 
The thick curve in Fig. 7 represents the pointwise average of aligned 
responses and is used for model identification.
Based on the average response, the remote-node dynamics are 
approximated by a second-order system with dead time. From 
Fig. 7, we estimate a static gain Km = 1, dead time Td = 10 s, 
overshoot of approximately 23% (corresponding to damping ratio

This follows the established practice of using low-order process 
models with delay to represent PRV–network dynamics around an 
operating point (Galuppini et al. 2019). The key difference is that 
the identification data are obtained directly from field step tests on 
the real sector, rather than from simulated perturbations. In addition, 
stochastic demand effects are represented separately through the 
stationary head-loss disturbance model, so Eq. (4) captures only the 
controllable PRV–remote-node dynamics.
The gain Km in Eq. (4) is treated as constant for linear controller 
design. In practice, PRV gain may vary with operating point; 
Janus and Ulanicki (2018) showed that electronically controlled 
PRVs can exhibit sharp gain increases near closure, leading to 
oscillations or instability. Here, experiments covered approximately 
17–45 m H2O downstream setpoints (xp ∈[17%, 45%]), well away 
from near-closure conditions. Over this range, step responses show 
consistent time constants and settling times, supporting the use of a 
single LTI model. For other valves or wider operating ranges, gain 
variation should be assessed and gain scheduling or nonlinear com-
pensation may be required. Recent RTC developments explicitly 
address gain variability by adjusting controller gain online while 
providing closed-loop stability guarantees, e.g., gain-scheduled 
internal model control (Galuppini et al. 2023b). Related evidence on 
low-flow/low-opening oscillatory behavior further motivates gain-
variation awareness in PRV control (Ulanicki and Skworcow 2014).

valve behavior, and remote-node dynamics.

Fig. 9 shows that the identified model reproduces measured

for controller design. Although the hydraulic system is nonlinear, 
a linear model identified in the main operating range is sufficiently 
accurate for controller synthesis, particularly because feedback with

Validation of the Dynamic and Stationary Model on a 
Typical Day
Fig. 10 compares model predictions and field data over 24 h for 
two independent days. Despite demand randomness, the model 
tracks daily pressure variations with errors within approximately 
±1 m H2O.
The 24-h profiles used for validation were not employed during 
identification, so these tests provide independent assessments of 
predictive capability. Systematic error patterns are attributable to 
unmodeled nonlinearities, but the RST controller includes integral 
action to compensate for low-frequency mismatch and disturban-
ces. Maintaining remote-node pressure within a narrow band of 
±1 m H2O over a full day supports the adequacy of the model for 
controller synthesis.
Design of the Pressure Regulation Controller at the 
Remote Node
We designed a digital controller combining a Smith predictor and an 
RST structure. The Smith predictor compensates for process dead 
time, while the RST controller provides disturbance rejection and 
reference tracking. Similar classical feedback designs have been 
applied to PRV control, including integral controllers with Smith 
predictors (Fontana et al. 2018b) and linear optimal regulators 
(Galuppini et al. 2019). The contribution here is the complete 
field-driven workflow from data collection and model identification 
to explicit computation of implementable RST polynomials.
The controller structure is shown in Fig. 11. The blocks Tc and 
GT correspond to tracking dynamics, while Rc and Sc correspond to 
regulation. Gp(z) denotes the delay-free discrete process model used 
inside the Smith predictor, while the separate block z−n represents 
the transport delay in sampling periods. The upper disturbance 
input is the equivalent head-loss term hl, obtained from the static 
relation hl(qin)
A Smith predictor is appropriate when the internal model cap-
tures the dominant delay and dynamics with sufficient accuracy. In 
this study, the identified low-order model is validated against inde-
pendent field data, yielding 24-h prediction errors within approxi-
mately ±1 m H2O at the remote node (Fig. 10), which supports

FILE Ahmed_2025_Windowed_LSTM_Autoencoder.pdf PAGES 6
PAGE 2 CCWI 2025 - 21st Computing & Control for the Water Industry Conference, Sheffield, UK 
 
imbalance, further reducing the effectiveness of supervised learning 
approaches in operational settings. 
 
These limitations make semi-supervised methods, and autoencoders (AEs) in 
particular, a promising alternative. AEs learn the typical patterns of normal 
operation and can detect anomalies (i.e. leaks) by measuring how poorly new 
data is reconstructed [5], [6]. Since they only require normal data for training, 
they are well-suited for environments where labelled leak data is unavailable. 
AEs and other neural networks can use various architectures. Long Short-Term 
Memory (LSTM) networks are particularly well-suited for this task, as they can 
capture complex temporal dependencies inherent in water time-series data, 
especially crucial for identifying slow-evolving leakage patterns.  
 
However, LSTM models come with increased computational complexity, raising 
concerns around training time and deployability. To evaluate this trade-off, we 
compare our proposed LSTM-based multivariate autoencoder to a 1D 
Convolutional Neural Network (CNN)-based autoencoder trained under the 
same conditions. The 1D CNN offers faster training, lower memory 
requirements, and is easier to deploy in real-time systems where resources may 
be limited [7], [8], By benchmarking these two architectures, we aim to assess 
whether the improved temporal modelling of LSTMs justifies their added 
complexity in detecting incipient leakages, guiding utilities toward the most 
suitable solution for their infrastructure and operational context. 
 
LEAK DETECTION APPROACH 
We propose a Windowed LSTM-based Multivariate AE for detecting incipient 
leaks in WDNs and compare it to a 1D CNN-based AE, both trained using only 
normal operational data. The autoencoders are designed to reconstruct input 
data sequences. When they encounter patterns that deviate from what they 
have learned, like those caused by a leak, they produce a higher reconstruction 
error. By comparing this error to normal levels, we can identify anomalies 
without needing explicit labels for leaks. 
 
We use multivariate inputs by combining data from several sensors. This adds 
robustness, since it is unlikely all sensors would drift or malfunction at the same 
time. To handle time-dependent patterns and make training more manageable, 
we segment the data into daily windows. This captures daily periodicity in water 
usage and helps the models learn more efficiently. 
 
For evaluation, both models are tested on DMA C of the BattleDIM dataset [9], 
which is a hydraulically isolated part of the network with irregular demand 
patterns. The available sensors in this district include three pressure sensors 
(n1, n4, and n31), a tank level sensor (T1), and a flow meter (PUMP_1). Time 
series data from 2018 and 2019 is pre-processed and standardized before 
being split into training sets (containing only normal data) and test sets 
(including both normal and leakage conditions). 
 
Model performance is assessed based on reconstruction error and time to 
detection. Most standard anomaly metrics are designed for singular anomalies 
[10], making them unsuitable for evaluating cumulative leaks. We therefore 
introduce a new metric: the factor of mean reconstruction error (MRE), to 
address this limitation. This factor is calculated by taking the average 

PAGE 3 CCWI 2025 - 21st Computing & Control for the Water Industry Conference, Sheffield, UK 
 
reconstruction error for a leak state and dividing it by the average reconstruction 
error of the normal state. A higher MRE indicates the leak is easier to detect, 
quantifying how clearly the model can distinguish leak events from normal 
behaviour. 
 
EXPERIMENTAL SETUP 
Data Preprocessing 
A moving z-score standardization method [11] is applied to standardize each 
sensor’s data. A window size corresponding to one month is chosen to account 
for gradual changes in flow and pressure measurements caused by seasonal 
variations. Failing to account for these gradual changes can result in a higher 
number of false positives, as flow and pressure patterns in water distribution 
networks (WDNs) differ significantly between seasons. 
 
DMA C contains a persistent leak (p257) that starts in early 2018 and lasts until 
the end of 2019. In 2018, another leak (p31) also occurs mid-year, and is fixed. 
In order to include 2018 data, and therefore account for seasonality of the data, 
time series are selected as follows: 
• first train set - from peak of p257 to start of p31 leak (’2018-01-26 00:00:00’ 
to ’2018-06-28 23:55:00’) 
• second train set - from after p31 leak was fixed until mid-January 2019 
(”2018-08-13 00:00:00’ to ’2019-0114 23:55:00’) 
• test set - from mid-January 2019 until end of 2019 (’201901-15 00:00:00’ 
onwards) 
 
This method treats the p257 leak as part of the normal baseline, given that it is 
present and consistent throughout the selected dataset. 
 
The multivariate data is then segmented for sequence-to-sequence model 
implementation. An overlapping sliding window approach [12] is used for this 
segmentation. A window size of 288 is selected, representing a 24-hour period 
with a data frequency of 5 minutes. This overlapping window structure aims to 
decompose the entire time series into 288 unique patterns that repeat 
consistently throughout the time series.  
 
The segmentation is done for each aforementioned set, and only after this are 
the two train sets combined. This is done to ensure no windows exist across the 
edges of the two sets, as such windows would not be representative of reality. 
15% of the data is randomly split from the train set to create a validation set, 
which is used to finetune the hyperparameters of the models. 
 
Model Training 
The two models were defined as shown in Figure 1, with the CNN having 
Average Pooling (size 2) and Dropout (0.2) applied after each encoder & 
decoder layers. Tanh activation functions were used in both Layer 1’s.  
 
The parameters in each of the autoencoders are optimized such that the 
Measure Squared Error (MSE) between the input multivariate data and the 
reconstructed data is minimal. This is achieved using an Adam optimiser with a 
learning rate of 0.001. The models were determined to have trained sufficiently 
after 10 epochs. 

PAGE 4 CCWI 2025 - 21st Computing & Control for the Water Industry Conference, Sheffield, UK 
 
 
Figure 1. Dimensions of Data at each layer of the CNN (magenta) and LSTM 
(green) autoencoders 
 
The test data is used to evaluate the models on reconstruction error, factors of 
mean reconstruction and time to detection. A change point algorithm is required 
for the latter. Binary change point detection performs fast signal segmentation 
using a sequential approach: it identifies a change point in the signal, splits the 
series at that point, and repeats the process on the resulting sub-signals. This 
method offers low complexity, extends single change point detection to multiple 
points, and works without prior knowledge of the number of regimes. 
 
For this use case, it detected similar change points to the more robust linearly 
penalized segmentation (PELT) [13] algorithm, and at a significantly faster rate; 
over 100 times faster in most cases. Only positive change points are considered 
for raising the alarm that a leak has occurred. Negative change points would 
indicate the fixing of a leak, but no such instances occur in our test dataset. 
 
EVALUATION AND DISCUSSION 
Reconstruction Error 
As shown in Figure 2, the Windowed LSTM AE has the lowest reconstruction 
loss for the normal state. The deviation of the reconstruction errors from the 
median is also relatively low, indicating reliable reconstruction of normal data. 
 
Figure 2. Reconstruction Error for Normal State Data 
 
Factors of Mean Reconstruction Error 
Table 1 represents the average reconstruction error for each architecture. As can 
be seen, the Windowed LSTM AE not only produced the lowest mean 
reconstruction error for the normal state, but also the highest deviation factors for 
both single leak and multi-leak situations.

PAGE 5 CCWI 2025 - 21st Computing & Control for the Water Industry Conference, Sheffield, UK 
 
Time to Detection (TTD) 
Figure 3 shows that both abrupt and incipient leaks cause a significant rise in 
reconstruction error, aiding the Binary Segmentation change point detector. In 
DMA C, no leaks occurred from January 1 to February 9, 2019, resulting in 
minimal reconstruction error. Two leaks followed: an abrupt leak on February 10 
at 13:05 and an incipient leak on May 30 at 21:55, both persisting until December 
31. The change point detector successfully identified both leaks using 
reconstruction errors from both algorithms. For the CNN AE, a penalty of 10 and 
a period of a fortnight was selected, while for the LSTM AE, a penalty of 3 with a 
period of a month was used. 
 
 
Figure 3. Comparison of TTD for Leak 1 (Abrupt) and Leak 2 (Incipient) 
 
The CNN AE exhibited a detection lag of over four days for the incipient leak, 
whereas the LSTM AE detected it in under two days. Incipient leak detection 
depends on its growth rate—exponential growth leads to faster identification, 
while linear growth causes delays. The May 30 leak reached peak flow on August 
11, therefore all interim alarms are considered part of the same event. A single 
false alarm was recorded for the CNN AE after this period.

Manage., 2026, 152(8): 05026021 \\r\\n Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 09/20/26. \\r\\nCopyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. \\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 7 Fig. 9. Remote-node pressure model validation against field \\r\\nmeasurements.                                                                                        \\r\\n(a)\\r\\n(b)\\r\\n(c)\\r\\n(d)\\r\\nFig. 10. Validation of the combined disturbance and dynamic models over a typical 24-h operation.\\r\\nmeasured hd(t) and hm(t). Validation data were collected between \\r\\n17:30 and 18:00, when demand is relatively stable. A measured \\r\\ninflow time series, qin(t), was used as the disturbance input. These \\r\\ndata are independent of the identification dataset.\\r\\nFig. 9 shows that the identified model reproduces measured \\r\\nremote-node dynamics with small deviations that are acceptable \\r\\nfor controller design. Although the hydraulic system is nonlinear, \\r\\na linear model identified in the main operating range is sufficiently \\r\\naccurate for controller synthesis, particularly because feedback with \\r\\nintegral action compensates for steady-state mismatch.\\r\\nValidation of the Dynamic and Stationary Model on a \\r\\nTypical Day\\r\\nFig. 10 compares model predictions and field data over 24 h for \\r\\ntwo independent days. Despite demand randomness, the model \\r\\ntracks daily pressure variations with errors within approximately \\r\\n±1 m H2O.\\r\\nThe 24-h profiles used for validation were not employed during \\r\\nidentification, so these tests provide independent assessments of \\r\\npredictive capability. Systematic error patterns are attributable to \\r\\nunmodeled nonlinearities, but the RST controller includes integral \\r\\naction to compensate for low-frequency mismatch and disturban-\\r\\nces. Maintaining remote-node pressure within a narrow band of \\r\\n±1 m H2O over a full day supports the adequacy of the model for \\r\\ncontroller synthesis.\\r\\nDesign of the Pressure Regulation Controller at the \\r\\nRemote Node\\r\\nWe designed a digital controller combining a Smith predictor and an \\r\\nRST structure. The Smith predictor compensates for process dead \\r\\ntime, while the RST controller provides disturbance rejection and \\r\\nreference tracking. Similar classical feedback designs have been \\r\\napplied to PRV control, including integral controllers with Smith \\r\\npredictors (Fontana et al. 2018b) and linear optimal regulators \\r\\n(Galuppini et al. 2019). The contribution here is the complete \\r\\nfield-driven workflow from data collection and model identification \\r\\nto explicit computation of implementable RST polynomials.\\r\\nThe controller structure is shown in Fig. 11. The blocks Tc and \\r\\nGT correspond to tracking dynamics, while Rc and Sc correspond to \\r\\nregulation. Gp(z) denotes the delay-free discrete process model used \\r\\ninside the Smith predictor, while the separate block z−n represents \\r\\nthe transport delay in sampling periods. The upper disturbance \\r\\ninput is the equivalent head-loss term hl, obtained from the static \\r\\nrelation hl(qin)\\r\\nA Smith predictor is appropriate when the internal model cap-\\r\\ntures the dominant delay and dynamics with sufficient accuracy. In \\r\\nthis study, the identified low-order model is validated against inde-\\r\\npendent field data, yielding 24-h prediction errors within approxi-\\r\\nmately ±1 m H2O at the remote node (Fig. 10), which supports \\r\\n05026021-7\\r\\n© ASCE\\r\\nJ. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.\\r\\n J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2026, 152(8): 05026021 \\r\\n Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by New Jersey Institute of Technology on 09/20/26. \\r\\nCopyright ASCE; all rights reserved, including rights for text and data mining and training of artificial intelligence or similar technologies. \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE Ahmed_2025_Windowed_LSTM_Autoencoder.pdf PAGES 6\\r\\nPAGE 2 CCWI 2025 - 21st Computing & Control for the Water Industry Conference, Sheffield, UK \\r\\n \\r\\nimbalance, further reducing the effectiveness of supervised learning \\r\\napproaches in operational settings. \\r\\n \\r\\nThese limitations make semi-supervised methods, and autoencoders (AEs) in \\r\\nparticular, a promising alternative. AEs learn the typical patterns of normal \\r\\noperation and can detect anomalies (i.e. leaks) by measuring how poorly new \\r\\ndata is reconstructed [5], [6]. Since they only require normal data for training, \\r\\nthey are well-suited for environments where labelled leak data is unavailable. \\r\\nAEs and other neural networks can use various architectures. Long Short-Term \\r\\nMemory (LSTM) networks are particularly well-suited for this task, as they can \\r\\ncapture complex temporal dependencies inherent in water time-series data, \\r\\nespecially crucial for identifying slow-evolving leakage patterns.  \\r\\n \\r\\nHowever, LSTM models come with increased computational complexity, raising \\r\\nconcerns around training time and deployability. To evaluate this trade-off, we \\r\\ncompare our proposed LSTM-based multivariate autoencoder to a 1D \\r\\nConvolutional Neural Network (CNN)-based autoencoder trained under the \\r\\nsame conditions. The 1D CNN offers faster training, lower memory \\r\\nrequirements, and is easier to deploy in real-time systems where resources may \\r\\nbe limited [7], [8], By benchmarking these two architectures, we aim to assess \\r\\nwhether the improved temporal modelling of LSTMs justifies their added \\r\\ncomplexity in detecting incipient leakages, guiding utilities toward the most \\r\\nsuitable solution for their infrastructure and operational context. \\r\\n \\r\\nLEAK DETECTION APPROACH \\r\\nWe propose a Windowed LSTM-based Multivariate AE for detecting incipient \\r\\nleaks in WDNs and compare it to a 1D CNN-based AE, both trained using only \\r\\nnormal operational data. The autoencoders are designed to reconstruct input \\r\\ndata sequences. When they encounter patterns that deviate from what they \\r\\nhave learned, like those caused by a leak, they produce a higher reconstruction \\r\\nerror. By comparing this error to normal levels, we can identify anomalies \\r\\nwithout needing explicit labels for leaks. \\r\\n \\r\\nWe use multivariate inputs by combining data from several sensors. This adds \\r\\nrobustness, since it is unlikely all sensors would drift or malfunction at the same \\r\\ntime. To handle time-dependent patterns and make training more manageable, \\r\\nwe segment the data into daily windows. This captures daily periodicity in water \\r\\nusage and helps the models learn more efficiently. \\r\\n \\r\\nFor evaluation, both models are tested on DMA C of the BattleDIM dataset [9], \\r\\nwhich is a hydraulically isolated part of the network with irregular demand \\r\\npatterns. The available sensors in this district include three pressure sensors \\r\\n(n1, n4, and n31), a tank level sensor (T1), and a flow meter (PUMP_1). Time \\r\\nseries data from 2018 and 2019 is pre-processed and standardized before \\r\\nbeing split into training sets (containing only normal data) and test sets \\r\\n(including both normal and leakage conditions). \\r\\n \\r\\nModel performance is assessed based on reconstruction error and time to \\r\\ndetection. Most standard anomaly metrics are designed for singular anomalies \\r\\n[10], making them unsuitable for evaluating cumulative leaks. We therefore \\r\\nintroduce a new metric: the factor of mean reconstruction error (MRE), to \\r\\naddress this limitation. This factor is calculated by taking the average \\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 3 CCWI 2025 - 21st Computing & Control for the Water Industry Conference, Sheffield, UK \\r\\n \\r\\nreconstruction error for a leak state and dividing it by the average reconstruction \\r\\nerror of the normal state. A higher MRE indicates the leak is easier to detect, \\r\\nquantifying how clearly the model can distinguish leak events from normal \\r\\nbehaviour. \\r\\n \\r\\nEXPERIMENTAL SETUP \\r\\nData Preprocessing \\r\\nA moving z-score standardization method [11] is applied to standardize each \\r\\nsensor’s data. A window size corresponding to one month is chosen to account \\r\\nfor gradual changes in flow and pressure measurements caused by seasonal \\r\\nvariations. Failing to account for these gradual changes can result in a higher \\r\\nnumber of false positives, as flow and pressure patterns in water distribution \\r\\nnetworks (WDNs) differ significantly between seasons. \\r\\n \\r\\nDMA C contains a persistent leak (p257) that starts in early 2018 and lasts until \\r\\nthe end of 2019. In 2018, another leak (p31) also occurs mid-year, and is fixed. \\r\\nIn order to include 2018 data, and therefore account for seasonality of the data, \\r\\ntime series are selected as follows: \\r\\n• first train set - from peak of p257 to start of p31 leak (’2018-01-26 00:00:00’ \\r\\nto ’2018-06-28 23:55:00’) \\r\\n• second train set - from after p31 leak was fixed until mid-January 2019 \\r\\n(”2018-08-13 00:00:00’ to ’2019-0114 23:55:00’) \\r\\n• test set - from mid-January 2019 until end of 2019 (’201901-15 00:00:00’ \\r\\nonwards) \\r\\n \\r\\nThis method treats the p257 leak as part of the normal baseline, given that it is \\r\\npresent and consistent throughout the selected dataset. \\r\\n \\r\\nThe multivariate data is then segmented for sequence-to-sequence model \\r\\nimplementation. An overlapping sliding window approach [12] is used for this \\r\\nsegmentation. A window size of 288 is selected, representing a 24-hour period \\r\\nwith a data frequency of 5 minutes. This overlapping window structure aims to \\r\\ndecompose the entire time series into 288 unique patterns that repeat \\r\\nconsistently throughout the time series.  \\r\\n \\r\\nThe segmentation is done for each aforementioned set, and only after this are \\r\\nthe two train sets combined. This is done to ensure no windows exist across the \\r\\nedges of the two sets, as such windows would not be representative of reality. \\r\\n15% of the data is randomly split from the train set to create a validation set, \\r\\nwhich is used to finetune the hyperparameters of the models. \\r\\n \\r\\nModel Training \\r\\nThe two models were defined as shown in Figure 1, with the CNN having \\r\\nAverage Pooling (size 2) and Dropout (0.2) applied after each encoder & \\r\\ndecoder layers. Tanh activation functions were used in both Layer 1’s.  \\r\\n \\r\\nThe parameters in each of the autoencoders are optimized such that the \\r\\nMeasure Squared Error (MSE) between the input multivariate data and the \\r\\nreconstructed data is minimal. This is achieved using an Adam optimiser with a \\r\\nlearning rate of 0.001. The models were determined to have trained sufficiently \\r\\nafter 10 epochs. \\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 4 CCWI 2025 - 21st Computing & Control for the Water Industry Conference, Sheffield, UK \\r\\n \\r\\n \\r\\nFigure 1. Dimensions of Data at each layer of the CNN (magenta) and LSTM \\r\\n(green) autoencoders \\r\\n \\r\\nThe test data is used to evaluate the models on reconstruction error, factors of \\r\\nmean reconstruction and time to detection. A change point algorithm is required \\r\\nfor the latter. Binary change point detection performs fast signal segmentation \\r\\nusing a sequential approach: it identifies a change point in the signal, splits the \\r\\nseries at that point, and repeats the process on the resulting sub-signals. This \\r\\nmethod offers low complexity, extends single change point detection to multiple \\r\\npoints, and works without prior knowledge of the number of regimes. \\r\\n \\r\\nFor this use case, it detected similar change points to the more robust linearly \\r\\npenalized segmentation (PELT) [13] algorithm, and at a significantly faster rate; \\r\\nover 100 times faster in most cases. Only positive change points are considered \\r\\nfor raising the alarm that a leak has occurred. Negative change points would \\r\\nindicate the fixing of a leak, but no such instances occur in our test dataset. \\r\\n \\r\\nEVALUATION AND DISCUSSION \\r\\nReconstruction Error \\r\\nAs shown in Figure 2, the Windowed LSTM AE has the lowest reconstruction \\r\\nloss for the normal state. The deviation of the reconstruction errors from the \\r\\nmedian is also relatively low, indicating reliable reconstruction of normal data. \\r\\n \\r\\nFigure 2. Reconstruction Error for Normal State Data \\r\\n \\r\\nFactors of Mean Reconstruction Error \\r\\nTable 1 represents the average reconstruction error for each architecture. As can \\r\\nbe seen, the Windowed LSTM AE not only produced the lowest mean \\r\\nreconstruction error for the normal state, but also the highest deviation factors for \\r\\nboth single leak and multi-leak situations. \\r\\n \\r\\nTable 1. Factors of Mean Reconstruction Error \\r\\nModel \\r\\nNormal error \\r\\n1 leak factor \\r\\n2 leak factor \\r\\nCNN AE \\r\\n0.0732 \\r\\n2.4034 \\r\\n4.2370 \\r\\nLSTM AE \\r\\n0.0031 \\r\\n9.7775 \\r\\n16.9816 \\r\\n \\r\\n \\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 5 CCWI 2025 - 21st Computing & Control for the Water Industry Conference, Sheffield, UK \\r\\n \\r\\nTime to Detection (TTD) \\r\\nFigure 3 shows that both abrupt and incipient leaks cause a significant rise in \\r\\nreconstruction error, aiding the Binary Segmentation change point detector. In \\r\\nDMA C, no leaks occurred from January 1 to February 9, 2019, resulting in \\r\\nminimal reconstruction error. Two leaks followed: an abrupt leak on February 10 \\r\\nat 13:05 and an incipient leak on May 30 at 21:55, both persisting until December \\r\\n31. The change point detector successfully identified both leaks using \\r\\nreconstruction errors from both algorithms. For the CNN AE, a penalty of 10 and \\r\\na period of a fortnight was selected, while for the LSTM AE, a penalty of 3 with a \\r\\nperiod of a month was used. \\r\\n \\r\\n \\r\\nFigure 3. Comparison of TTD for Leak 1 (Abrupt) and Leak 2 (Incipient) \\r\\n \\r\\nThe CNN AE exhibited a detection lag of over four days for the incipient leak, \\r\\nwhereas the LSTM AE detected it in under two days. Incipient leak detection \\r\\ndepends on its growth rate—exponential growth leads to faster identification, \\r\\nwhile linear growth causes delays. The May 30 leak reached peak flow on August \\r\\n11, therefore all interim alarms are considered part of the same event. A single \\r\\nfalse alarm was recorded for the CNN AE after this period. \\r\\n \\r\\nTable 2. Time to Detection Comparison \\r\\nModel \\r\\nAbrupt leak (hh:mm) \\r\\nIncipient leak (hh:mm) \\r\\n1D CNN AE [3] \\r\\n12:40 \\r\\n93:50 \\r\\nLILA-PINN [4] \\r\\n22:05 \\r\\n369:35 \\r\\nCNN AE \\r\\n21:25 \\r\\n117:20 \\r\\nLSTM AE \\r\\n21:35 \\r\\n43:55 \\r\\n \\r\\n\\r\\n\"}"

---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:\\r\\n\\r\\n```python\\r\\nfrom pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter\\r\\nfrom pypdf.generic import NameObject\\r\\n\\r\\nreader = PdfReader(input_pdf)\\r\\nwriter = PdfWriter()\\r\\nwriter.clone_document_from_reader(reader)\\r\\n\\r\\n# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.\\r\\nwriter.reattach_fields()\\r\\nfields = writer.get_fields() or {}\\r\\nmissing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)\\r\\nif missing:\\r\\n    raise ValueError(f\\\"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}\\\")\\r\\n\\r\\nvalues_to_write = dict(expected_values)\\r\\nif flatten:\\r\\n    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.\\r\\n    values_to_write = {\\r\\n        name: field.get(\\\"/V\\\", \\\"/Off\\\" if field.get(\\\"/FT\\\") == \\\"/Btn\\\" else \\\"\\\")\\r\\n        for name, field in fields.items()\\r\\n    }\\r\\n    values_to_write.update(expected_values)\\r\\n\\r\\nwriter.update_page_form_field_values(\\r\\n    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten\\r\\n)\\r\\n\\r\\nif flatten:\\r\\n    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.\\r\\n    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes=\\\"/Widget\\\")\\r\\n    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject(\\\"/AcroForm\\\"), None)\\r\\n\\r\\nwith open(output_pdf, \\\"wb\\\") as stream:\\r\\n    writer.write(stream)\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.\\r\\n5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Temp And Output Conventions\\r\\n\\r\\n- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.\\r\\n- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.\\r\\n- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Dependencies\\r\\n\\r\\nPrefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`\\r\\n- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler\\r\\n\\r\\nIf a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.\\r\\n\\r\\nPython packages:\\r\\n\\r\\n```bash\\r\\nuv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nIf `uv` is unavailable:\\r\\n\\r\\n```bash\\r\\npython3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nSystem tools for rendering:\\r\\n\\r\\nSwitched to a new branch 'audit/engineering-rationale-2026-10-28'\\n\"}"
  }
]
````

# Historical search, screening and grouping

## What is reproducible

The committed CSVs reproduce **Figure 4 of report v0.30**: 47 source records assigned to
35 related-work groups, with categories 27 leak/localization, 3 current-state estimation,
2 forecasting, 2 anomaly/fault detection and 1 sensor placement. Six groups were selected
for detailed discussion. Papers, versions and author repositories describing one method
are grouped rather than counted independently.

`inventory_sources.csv` retains every source ID, original URL, evidence locator and saved
checksum/commit from the historical inventory. `group_assignments.csv` makes all group
IDs, categories and selected/not-selected decisions visible. `repro/screening.py` joins the
files and recomputes counts. The code does not contain the category totals as its counting rule.

## Historical collection strategy

The earlier local search record was an exploratory, AI-assisted literature collection,
not a systematic review or a Scopus/Web of Science bibliometric study. It started from
the BattLeDIM committee, official dataset, benchmark paper and competition reports;
followed citations and author code; then searched for pressure prediction/reconstruction,
graphs, autoencoders, dictionaries, ensembles and related evaluations.

Representative queries retained from that earlier search record include:

- `BattLeDIM L-Town leak detection localization github benchmark`
- `L-Town pressure prediction forecasting github BattLeDIM`
- `"BattLeDIM" "pressure forecasting"`
- `"L-Town" "pressure" "forecasting" learning`
- `"BattLeDIM" "15-minute" forecasting`
- `"BattLeDIM" "github" "autoencoder"`
- `"SAM-kNN Regressor for Online Learning" pdf`

The counted set retains original or explicitly modified L-Town studies. Other networks,
general reviews and tools are not in the historical denominator. A method's primary goal
determines its category even if pressure forecasting is an intermediate diagnostic step.
This is a scholarly classification, not a result established by counting code.

## Limits and post-feedback addition

Search engines and publisher availability change; a complete historical result log was
not retained. The collection and classifications are not independently human-validated
merely because the CSV joins pass. The inventory does not establish worldwide priority.

**Ahmed et al. (2025), DOI 10.15131/shef.data.29921204.v1, was missing from the historical
inventory.** Its PDF/link is listed as a post-feedback addition in the literature index; it
is not silently inserted into the 35-group historical count. A future literature revision
must reassess grouping and selection in light of that paper.

no implicit unit conversion.')\n    elevations = {r[0]: float(r[1]) for r in sections['[JUNCTIONS]']}\n    graph = defaultdict(list)\n    for row in sections['[PIPES]']:\n        if len(row) > 7 and row[7].upper() == 'CLOSED':\n            continue\n        for left, right in [(row[1], row[2]), (row[2], row[1])]:\n            graph[left].append((right, float(row[3])))\n\n    def component(root):\n        seen, stack = set(), [root]\n        while stack:\n            node = stack.pop()\n            if node not in seen:\n                seen.add(node)\n                stack.extend(peer for peer, _ in graph[node] if peer not in seen)\n        return seen\n\n    c_nodes = component('T1') & elevations.keys()\n    queue, distances = [(0.0, 'T1')], {}\n    while queue:\n        distance, node = heapq.heappop(queue)\n        if node in distances:\n            continue\n        distances[node] = distance\n        for peer, length in graph[node]:\n            if peer not in distances:\n                heapq.heappush(queue, (distance + length, peer))\n    sensors = [n for n in config['pressure_sensors'] if n in c_nodes]\n    maximum = max(elevations[n] for n in c_nodes)\n    sectors = {}\n    for name, root in [('A', 'n300'), ('B', 'n226'), ('C', 'T1')]:\n        nodes = component(root)\n        sectors[name] = {\n            'junction_count': len(nodes & elevations.keys()),\n            'pressure_sensors': [n for n in config['pressure_sensors'] if n in nodes],\n            'AMR_count': len(nodes & set(config['amrs'])),\n        }\n    return {\n        'units_option': units,\n        'elevation_units': 'metres in model vertical reference; ## Numerical audit and rendering audit are different

`verification.json` compares freshly recomputed values against the historical values
in `provenance/expected_report_values.json`. `run_manifest.json` records exact bytes, code,
packages and output hashes. Historical report figures are preserved in
`provenance/figures_as_reported/`. The regenerated plots use the same data and calculations;
they are not advertised as pixel-identical copies.

## Screening boundaries

Recreating Figure 4 means calculating counts from the complete published inventory. It does
not mean rerunning a 2026 search engine or proving that every relevant work was found.
Read [the screening protocol](../literature/SEARCH_AND_SCREENING.md), especially its
historical omission of Ahmed et al. (2025).

name: computer-use
description: Control Windows apps from ChatGPT

# Computer Use

Use this skill to automate the UI of Microsoft Windows apps. It uses SendInput, UI Automation, and Windows.Graphics.Capture screenshots that work even when windows are occluded.

If this plugin is available, read this entire `SKILL.md` once before Windows automation work, before saying Computer Use is unavailable, and before falling back to other Windows automation.

Start with the directions in the Initialize section below. Read these bundled Markdown files relative to this `SKILL.md` when you need information about the specific topic they cover:

- `../../docs/guidance.md`: core runtime behavior, target-window workflow, screenshot handling, and recovery guidance. You MUST read this before controlling Windows apps.
- `../../docs/api.md`: full `sky` API reference. Read this when you need method signatures or object shapes.
- `../../docs/confirmations.md`: you MUST read this before deciding whether a Windows UI action needs confirmation

## Initialize

The bundled `cua_node` `@oai/sky` package is the core entry point for Computer Use. Import it directly by package name from the JavaScript session. Do not spawn `codex-computer-use.exe`, search for the helper executable, or build a custom helper protocol client.

Run this once per fresh `node_repl` JavaScript session:

```js
if (!globalThis.sky) {
  const { sky } = await import("@oai/sky");
  globalThis.sky = sky;
}
```

- Feedback marked Dataset/Task and Kaggle Contributions Very Good but Anticipated Contributions and Presentation Problematic because figures, correlations, persistence errors, topology checks, and â€œ35 groupsâ€ screening lacked code/notebooks and a reproducible trail; implementation was not the missing requirement. [Task 1]
- `web/scripts/revise_milestone2_v011.py` generates figures/correlations; `web/scripts/audit_feature_selection_v013.py` audits topology/distances. Read-only recalculation reproduced 78,624 rows, 78,621 pairs, persistence MAE 0.05177624298851451 m and RMSE 0.06849175638676988 m. [Task 1]

CCWI 2025 - 21st Computing & Control for the Water Industry Conference, Sheffield, UK 
 
Windowed Multivariate LSTM Autoencoder for Leak 
Detection in Water Distribution Networks 
 
Samer Ahmed1,2*, Dilek Düştegör1, Alexander Lazovik1, Heinrich Wörtche2,3 
1 Bernoulli Institute, University of Groningen, Groningen, The Netherlands 
2 Sensors & Smart Systems, Hanzehogeschool, Groningen, The Netherlands 
3 Eindhoven University of Technology, Eindhoven, The Netherlands 
*samer.ahmed@rug.nl 
 
ABSTRACT  
Leakages in water distribution networks (WDNs) are challenging to accurately 
identify and label, often leading to significant water loss and high repair costs. 
Building on existing unsupervised leak detection methods, this work introduces 
a Windowed LSTM-based multivariate autoencoder designed to improve the 
detection of incipient leaks in WDNs. We compare its performance with a 
similarly trained 1D CNN autoencoder using the BattleDIM dataset (DMA C), 
which features irregular demand patterns. The models are evaluated in terms of 
normal reconstruction error, factors of mean reconstruction error (a novel 
metric), and time to detection. Results show that the Windowed LSTM 
autoencoder achieves lower normal reconstruction error, higher deviation during 
leak events, and faster detection of incipient leaks compared to the 1D CNN 
baseline. 
 
Keywords: Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM), Water Distribution Network, 
Leakage Detection 
 
INTRODUCTION 
Leaks in water distribution networks (WDNs) are a common and costly issue, 
especially in aging infrastructures. Incipient leaks often start slow and gradually 
grow worse over time, making them hard to notice. While catching them early 
can save water and money, most existing detection methods are designed for 
abrupt leaks (which are sudden and obvious) [1], [2]. When applied to incipient 
leaks, these techniques either take too long to respond [3], [4], or trigger too 
many false alarms, both of which can be expensive and disruptive for utilities. 
This highlights the need for more robust approaches specifically tailored to 
reduce the runtime of incipient leakages, enabling quicker detection and 
mitigation. 
 
Many existing approaches rely on statistical models, but these methods often 
assume that the data follows a clean, Gaussian distribution. In reality, water 
networks data is typically non-Gaussian, seasonal, and non-stationary. As a 
result, statistical models often produce high false-positive rates when applied in 
practice [3], making them unreliable for real-time detection of incipient leaks. 
Supervised machine learning methods, such as Decision Trees and Random 
Forests, offer more flexibility and can handle non-linearities in the data. 
However, these models require large, labelled datasets that include both normal 
and leak conditions. Generating such datasets is extremely challenging in real-
world water networks, as it would require highly accurate hydraulic models. 
Additionally, leak 
CCWI 2025 - 21st Computing & Control for the Water Industry Conference, Sheffield, UK 
 
Table 2 shows the results for the two models and compares them with SOTA 
models from literature [3], [4]. The LSTM autoencoder significantly reduces the 
detection time for incipient leaks and performs comparably to other methods in 
detecting abrupt leaks, where a simple threshold-based approach is used. 
 
CONCLUSION 
The Windowed LSTM autoencoder demonstrated improved performance in 
detecting incipient leaks, achieving faster detection times while reducing false 
alarms. Future work could involve evaluating this method on additional datasets 
to enable broader comparisons against other established benchmarks.

Nominal shortest pipe lengths are not transport delays, hydraulic sensitivities or evidence of sensor value.\n+\n+Reproduce the optional generator check after the ordinary data download:\n+\n+```powershell\n+.\\.venv\\Scripts\\python.exe scripts/audit_engineering_rationale.py --download-generator\n+```\n+\n+This downloads an additional 177 MB into ignored `data/raw/`. A verified cache can instead be passed with `--generator path/to/L-TOWN_Real.inp`. Only static sections are retained; no forecast is evaluated. [Student questions](STUDENT_REVIEW_LOG.md) distinguish agreement with this rationale from actual personal execution.\n*** Add File: D:\\GitHub\\ML NJIT\\MiniProject\\DS675_MiniProject\\docs\\CRITICAL_EDA_DISCUSSION.md\n+# Critical interpretation of the descriptive EDA\n+\n+Post-feedback addition, 2026-10-28. AI-assisted draft interpretation pending student review. **Observed** below means recomputed from the official simulated measurements; it does not mean observed field performance.\n+\n+## What the completed analysis shows\n+\n+The [statistics](../results/reproduced/eda_statistics.json), produced by `describe` in [analysis.py](../repro/analysis.py), use January-September 2018: 78,624 timestamps, 273 complete days and 78,621 valid n1(+15) pairs. Persistence predicts the current n1 pressure unchanged. Its MAE is **0.05177624 m = 5.177624 cm** and RMSE **0.06849176 m = 6.849176 cm**. These are descriptive errors over that period, not future test performance. The n1 range is 25.67-29.36 m; a range is not a variability or uncertainty estimate.\n+\n+The small error leaves little absolute room for improvement. As an **illustration, not a result**, reducing this MAE by 10% saves only 0.0051776 m, or 0.51776 cm. Future comparisons must report both absolute and relative changes, using the baseline for the same evaluation period. A percentage alone can overstate practical importance; no operational significance threshold has been established. Not beating persistence would be an informative negative result.\n+\n+| Origin-time signal | Pearson correlation with n1 at +15 min | Interpretation to test, not an established benefit |\n+|---|---:|---|\n+| n1 | 0.992310 | Strong short-horizon dependence explains the competitive persistence baseline. |\n+| n4 | 0.975178 | Similar pressure behavior could help or be redundant with n1 history. |\n+| n31 | 0.970919 | High marginal association does not show independent information. |\n+| T1 | 0.923038 | Consistent with common storage-related behavior; incremental value is unknown. |\n+| PUMP_1 | -0.036721 | Weak full-period linear association does not rule out conditional or lagged effects. |\n+| AMR_n1 | -0.157378 | Modest negative association is not proof of a demand-driven causal effect. |\n+\n+These are current-signal versus **future-label** entries, not all current-current correlations. See [the full matrix](../results/reproduced/correlations.csv). Temporal dependence, shared daily cycles, seasonal changes and common operating conditions complicate interpretation. No independence assumption, correlation significance test or causal conclusion is asserted.\n+\n+## What remains a hypothesis\n+\n+Additional pressure sensors may mainly repeat n1 information. Tank level may describe a shared state that n1 already encodes. Pump inflow may add information near switching, although level-based pump control also makes pump flow a response to storage state. AMR_n1 may reflect local demand changes or may add noise/redundancy. Only matched feature comparisons can distinguish these possibilities; see [E0-E4](EXPERIMENTAL_CONTRIBUTION_PLAN.md).\n+\n+The [891.3208/573.7565/307.7926 m nominal paths](../results/reproduced/topology_audit.json) and higher monitored elevation motivate selection, not timing or sensitivity claims. [Generator differences](SECTOR_AND_NODE_SELECTION_RATIONALE.md) reinforce why static geometry must not be treated as measured dynamics. Tank buffering does not prove upstream independence.\n+\n+The data contain simulated leak regimes. Ahmed et al., [p. 3](../literature/papers/Ahmed_2025_Windowed_LSTM_Autoencoder.pdf#page=3), explicitly treat persistent p257 as part of their normal baseline. Our descriptive period is not certified leak-free. A chronological split may face changing regimes and seasons; generalization to real telemetry remains untested. No 2019 forecast or learned model has been evaluated.\n+\n+## Why daily overlays were useful\n+\n+The [273 daily profiles and clock-time means](../results/reproduced/figures/figure2_daily_profiles.png) make repeatable daily structure and day-to-day spread visible in little space. A clock-time mean hides dates and exceptional days and cannot establish predictive accuracy. The profile mean uses the full descriptive period; it is not a past-only deployment feature.\n+\n+The user-supplied rationale connects this design to prior field-data research. Full-text inspection confirms the same **general visual convention** in the ASCE article's Figure 4 (20 days), printed p. 05026021-5. Our BattLeDIM curves were recomputed, not copied; personal motivation remains awaiting review. See [the precise source/provenance distinction](PRIOR_RESEARCH_AND_METHOD_TRANSFER.md).\n*** Add File: D:\\GitHub\\ML NJIT\\MiniProject\\DS675_MiniProject\\docs\\AHMED_2025_COMPARISON.md\n+# Ahmed et al. (2025): shared measurements, different learning task\n+\n+Post-feedback source reading, 2026-10-28. AI-assisted comparison pending student review; published results were not reproduced. Source: Ahmed, Düştegör, Lazovik and Wörtche, *Windowed Multivariate LSTM Autoencoder for Leak Detection in Water Distribution Networks*, CCWI 2025, [DOI and original record](https://doi.org/10.15131/shef.data.29921204.v1), [local licensed six-page PDF](../literature/papers/Ahmed_2025_Windowed_LSTM_Autoencoder.pdf). Page numbers below are PDF pages.\n+\n+| Aspect | What the paper actually reports | Relation to this proposal |\n+|---|---|---|\n+| Data and signals, p. 2 | BattLeDIM DMA C, 2018/2019; n1, n4, n31, T1, PUMP_1. | These five signals were already combined in prior work. They cannot be claimed as a new five-signal selection. |\n+| Task, pp. 2-3 | Reconstruct multivariate input windows; elevated reconstruction error is used to detect leaks. Normal-operation training makes this a reconstruction/anomaly approach, not future-pressure supervised regression. | Our label is recorded n1 at t+15 min. AMR_n1 is a sixth candidate signal; its benefit remains untested. |\n+| Preparation, p. 3 | Moving z-score with a one-month window; overlapping windows of 288 five-minute samples (24 h). The two training segments are windowed separately before combination. | Use past-only lags, train-fitted preprocessing and explicit chronological boundaries; do not copy the paper's protocol as proof of causal deployment. |\n+| Models, pp. 3-4 / Fig. 1 | LSTM and 1D CNN autoencoders. Figure 1 shows LSTM encoded widths 64 then 32 and decoder widths 32 then 64, followed by reconstruction to 5x288. CNN uses pooling size 2 and dropout 0.2; the text specifies tanh in each model's first layer. Both minimize reconstruction MSE with Adam, learning rate 0.001, 10 epochs. | Course-scale regression is a simpler different task; no autoencoder implementation or accuracy replication is claimed here. |\n+| Detection, p. 4 | Binary segmentation on reconstruction errors, retaining positive changes for alarms; compared with PELT for change-point computation. | A time-to-detection result is not a +15-minute forecast horizon. |\n+| Metrics, pp. 2-5 | Normal reconstruction error; factor of mean reconstruction error (leak-state mean divided by normal-state mean); time to detection. | They are not forecast MAE/RMSE in metres and cannot be ranked against our persistence errors. |\n+\n+## Split and leak treatment\n+\n+Page 3 specifies training segments 2018-01-26 to 2018-06-28 and 2018-08-13 to 2019-01-14 (end times 23:55); test starts 2019-01-15. Date typography in the paper is normalized here. Persistent p257 is explicitly treated as part of normal baseline, while the mid-2018 p31 period is excluded. “Normal” therefore does not mean leak-free. Fifteen percent of the training windows are randomly separated for validation.\n+\n+Overlapping windows and random validation raise a risk of strong train/validation dependence; without author implementation details, this is a methodological caution rather than a proved data-leakage finding. The text does not establish whether moving z-score statistics are trailing or centered, nor an exact live-availability protocol. We do not infer those details. Page 5's phrase about no early-2019 leaks should be read alongside the explicit persistent-p257 baseline on p. 3; the paper does not support calling the full interval physically leak-free.\n+\n+## Published results and their meaning\n+\n+| Table / measure | CNN AE | LSTM AE | Meaning |\n+|---|---:|---:|---|\n+| Table 1, p. 4: normal error | 0.0732 | 0.0031 | Reconstruction loss on standardized signals, not pressure error in metres |\n+| Table 1: one-leak MRE factor | 2.4034 | 9.7775 | Larger ratio indicates stronger separation from the normal baseline |\n+| Table 1: two-leak MRE factor | 4.2370 | 16.9816 | Same ratio for the multi-leak state |\n+| Table 2, p. 5: abrupt TTD | 21 h 25 min | 21 h 35 min | LSTM is slightly slower for this abrupt event |\n+| Table 2: incipient TTD | 117 h 20 min | 43 h 55 min | LSTM detects this gradual event sooner than the paper's own CNN |\n+\n+Table 2 also lists a previous 1D CNN from another source (93 h 50 min for the incipient leak); it must not be confused with the paper's own CNN. These are author-reported results under their detection settings, not our measured performance.\n+\n+This close prior work narrows the defensible contribution: test **incremental information for future n1**, including a controlled AMR_n1 addition, rather than claim the shared sensors are new. Ahmed is a separate post-feedback addition; the historical 47-source/35-group chart remains frozen and qualified as a curated inventory. See [screening boundaries](../literature/SEARCH_AND_SCREENING.md).\n*** End Patch"));

      "type": "add",
      "content": "# From engineering reasoning to testable ML hypotheses\n\nPost-feedback addition, 2026-10-28. AI-assisted draft pending student review. The target is recorded **n1(t+15 min)**. Every predictor, transformation and inferred state must use only information available at origin t. Real measurement latency is an assumption to disclose, not a verified field property.\n\nFor a tank with water depth h and effective horizontal area A(h), continuity gives\n`A(h) dh/dt = Q_in - Q_out`, with consistent flow/time units. Pressure head at a connected junction also depends on elevation, losses and network state. These physical relations motivate features; they are not a fitted forecast model. T1 depth is not itself absolute water-surface elevation. PUMP_1 measures inflow, while unmeasured aggregate outflow remains essential. AMR_n1 measures nodal consumption in L/h, not total C outflow.\n\nThe [static audit](../results/engineering/model_geometry_verification.json) also shows level-based pump controls. Pump flow can respond to storage state as well as influence it. Consequently, a useful pump feature would not establish a one-way causal effect. Nominal/generator tank diameters differ; no geometry-based future level estimate is introduced here.\n\n| Hypothesis, still untested | Physical motivation and counterargument | Matched comparison / evidence against benefit |\n|---|---|---|\n| Peer pressures contain additional predictive information | n4/n31 may capture spatial variation; they may simply repeat n1's common daily pattern. | E2 vs E1, same method/rows. No stable validation/final gain contradicts the claimed benefit for this setup. |\n| Storage and pump signals help beyond pressure history | T1 describes storage and PUMP_1 one inflow; unknown outflow and feedback control complicate inference. | E3 vs E2. This tests the **block**, not a separate causal contribution from each signal. No gain is a valid result. |\n| Local demand improves the future-pressure prediction | AMR_n1 may describe local changes; it may be redundant or noisy. | E4 vs E3. An absent or unstable improvement argues against including this signal under the tested preparation/model. |\n| Improvements concentrate near operating changes | Weak overall pump correlation could conceal conditional effects. | Optional origin-time strata defined using training data, frozen on validation; no test-defined thresholds. Sparse strata or no gain weaken this hypothesis. |\n| Learning is useful at all for this horizon | Past pressure is highly informative but not perfectly constant. | E1-E4 vs E0 persistence. Failure to beat E0 means no demonstrated forecast benefit, not failure of conservation of mass. |\n\nSee the [experimental plan](EXPERIMENTAL_CONTRIBUTION_PLAN.md) for E0-E4. Failure to improve would not disprove every possible model or physical mechanism; conclusions are limited to this implementation and dataset. A block ablation cannot identify which member of the block helped without additional controlled tests.\n\nPersistence and a proposed previous-day same-clock forecast are simple sanity baselines. A calendar-only model could test how much apparent predictability comes from repeated daily cycles; it is optional, not a completed experiment. Any learned preprocessing, threshold, model or feature choice must be fitted/selected on training/validation only. No shuffled future samples or oracle future-change indicators may enter predictors.\n"
    },

      "content": "# Anticipated research contribution\n\nPost-feedback addition, 2026-10-28. **AI-assisted draft pending student review.** This statement proposes future work; it does not assert independent student implementation or already-demonstrated novelty.\n\nThe proposed contribution is a controlled assessment of **whether hydraulically selected measurements add useful information for forecasting recorded n1 pressure 15 minutes ahead, beyond n1 history and calendar information**. The project would compare increasingly rich input sets using the same regression methods, target timestamps, chronological periods and error measures. It would report both improvements and failures to improve, rather than claim a new algorithm.\n\nThe supplied engineering rationale favors a small tank-fed area with three monitored pressure nodes, measured tank level, pump inflow and a local demand measurement. [Static verification](SECTOR_AND_NODE_SELECTION_RATIONALE.md) supports that scope and n1's relative elevation/remoteness **among monitored nodes**. It does not establish a uniquely critical node or guaranteed feature value. The supplied prior-research context is documented in [method transfer](PRIOR_RESEARCH_AND_METHOD_TRANSFER.md); it is background, not evidence of current forecast performance.\n\n## What the experiments could add\n\n1. **A reproducible preparation protocol:** six signal series, past-only lags and known calendar values, explicit +15-minute labels and common eligible rows. Input availability is a timestamp-based assumption; real telemetry delays are not documented.\n2. **An incremental-value result:** distinguish the information in peer pressures, storage/pump signals and local AMR. The central question is not which variable has the highest marginal correlation, but which addition reduces future error after n1 history is already available.\n3. **An honest account of effect size:** compare MAE/RMSE in metres, centimetres and relative changes against the same-period persistence baseline. If gains are small, unstable or absent, report that outcome and the limits of the hydraulic selection argument.\n\n[Ahmed et al.](AHMED_2025_COMPARISON.md) already combine the five main C measurements for reconstruction-based leak detection. Our proposed future-pressure target and added AMR comparison are **extensions in task and evaluation**, not proof of worldwide first use. The historical literature inventory is incomplete and cannot establish priority.\n\n## What could contradict the rationale\n\nPersistence already has small descriptive error. Peer pressure signals may be redundant; T1 may describe state already visible in n1; pump flow may be useful only near operating changes or may add nothing; AMR_n1 may add noise. A high-elevation target may not be the most difficult or operationally important one. These are counterarguments the [matched experiments](EXPERIMENTAL_CONTRIBUTION_PLAN.md) must allow to win.\n\nExpected benefit is therefore conditional: a reproducible empirical answer about information value on this simulated network. No causal mechanism, field deployment benefit, error reduction, novelty-by-feature-combination or independent human authorship is claimed. Approval and personal reasoning must be supplied through the [student review log](STUDENT_REVIEW_LOG.md).\n"

      "content": "# Experimental contribution plan: proposed, not executed\n\nPost-feedback addition, 2026-10-28. AI-assisted draft pending student approval. **No learned model is trained in this documentation/audit task.**\n\n## Questions and controlled comparisons\n\n- **RQ1:** Can a small learned regressor improve +15-minute n1 MAE/RMSE over persistence on a period excluded from fitting and model selection?\n- **RQ2:** Do the selected hydraulic measurements improve prediction beyond n1 history, with identical models and eligible target timestamps?\n- **RQ3, optional:** Are improvements concentrated near predefined origin-time operating changes? Treat small-sample results cautiously.\n\n| Experiment | Inputs available at origin t | Comparison meaning |\n|---|---|---|\n| E0 | Current n1; predict unchanged | Persistence baseline |\n| E1, earlier comparison A | n1 lags + calendar | Learned history-only baseline |\n| E2 | E1 + n4/n31 lags | Incremental peer-pressure block |\n| E3, earlier comparison B | E2 + T1/PUMP_1 lags | Incremental storage/inflow block; five measured signal series shared with Ahmed |\n| E4, earlier comparison C | E3 + AMR_n1 lags | Incremental local-demand signal |\n\nThe A/B/C feature-set labels above are **not hydraulic Areas A/B/C**. Use E0-E4 in future outputs to avoid confusion. Six measured series become more than six columns after lags/calendar. E3 vs E2 tests a combined block; do not infer separate T1 and pump effects from that comparison.\n\nProposed manageable starting features: lags at 0, 5, 10, 15, 30 and 60 minutes for each included series, plus hour and day-of-week encoding. Start with Ridge as a linear comparator and a small Gradient Boosting regressor as the nonlinear candidate. Use the **same model family and settings across E1-E4** when isolating signal effects; fix a small settings grid and select a shared setting using validation only. Separately tuned best-model results may be secondary and must be labeled as a different comparison. Random Forest is deferred unless time permits. No architecture is claimed to be new or already tested.\n\n## Chronology and leakage controls\n\nTraining: January-September 2018. Validation: October-December 2018. After choosing and freezing preparation/settings, refit using 2018 and assess 2019 once under that protocol. The public reproduction already reads 2019 for structural checks; literature and configuration expose regime information. **2019 is not blind or untouched.** Record any other prior inspection in the student log. This is a final chronological assessment excluded from new model selection, with that limitation disclosed.\n\nConstruct labels explicitly at t+15 minutes on the five-minute grid. Keep an origin only if its label belongs to the same partition; exclude the final three origins at partition boundaries. Lags may use earlier partition history that would truly be available at the origin (e.g. December inputs for a January origin). First training rows without the full one-hour history are dropped. Fit scalers, imputation if ever needed, feature transforms and other learned preparation on training data only; after protocol freeze, refit them on 2018 before final assessment.\n\nUse the **intersection of valid target timestamps across all experiments**, including E0. Do not compare different rows because an input needs more lags. The existing descriptive 78,621-pair calculation has no one-hour lag filter; its count is not the future experiment's final sample count. Check timestamps explicitly rather than assuming a row shift remains valid if a gap appears. No centered rolling windows, future interpolation, true future tank level, future pump values or future demand may enter predictors. Timestamp availability is assumed; telemetry delay has not been measured.\n\n## Measures and decision rules\n\nReport MAE and RMSE in metres (also centimetres). For loss L, absolute gain is `L_baseline - L_model`; relative gain is `100*(L_baseline-L_model)/L_baseline`, where the denominator is positive and evaluated on the same rows. Compare E1-E4 against E0 and adjacent experiments. Report both metrics even if only one improves, and retain negative results. Validation MAE is the proposed primary model-selection measure; RMSE is secondary. A positive gain is evidence under this protocol, not a pre-established operationally meaningful benefit.\n\nIf feasible, report paired daily losses, error distributions and a day/block-based uncertainty analysis with its dependence assumptions. Random-row confidence intervals would ignore temporal dependence; blocks do not guarantee full independence either. Do not invent a precision requirement for a utility.\n\nOptional strata must be recorded **before final scoring**: for example, origins with absolute five-minute pump-flow change above the training 95th percentile of nonzero changes, or recent past 15-minute n1 change above a training quantile. Choose/validate the rule using 2018 only and freeze thresholds. Handle absent nonzero changes by omitting that stratum, not redefining it on 2019. If actual **future** pressure change is used for a retrospective error breakdown, label it an oracle/retrospective group; it cannot define a real-time feature or route a prediction. Do not use leak labels or future regimes for tuning.\n\n## Four-week scope and limits\n\nWeek 1: student review, freeze preparation/split/metrics, build aligned rows. Week 2: E0 and two small regression families with limited validation settings. Week 3: matched E1-E4 comparisons; optional strata only if core work is complete. Week 4: final protocol freeze, one final assessment, reproducibility checks and joint interpretation. These are proposed tasks, not assigned members or completed work.\n\nOne simulated network and two calendar years cannot establish field generalization or worldwide novelty. Regime changes, seasonal shifts, model dependence and previously visible 2019 context limit conclusions. The plan seeks a useful, falsifiable course-project result, including the possibility that additional measurements do not help.\n"
